\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\left\langle\hat{u},x_{i}\right\rangle\right|\left|\left\langle x_{i},(\theta-\hat{\theta})/\|\theta\|\right\rangle\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle u,x_{i}\right\rangle^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle x_{i},(\theta-\hat{\theta})/\|\theta\|\right\rangle^{2}}\leq 2\,\frac{\|\theta-\hat{\theta}\|}{\|\theta\|}\leq 4\varepsilon,

using (
D.11
). Furthermore, using Cauchy-Schwarz readily gives

\mathbb{E}\left(\left|\left\langle\hat{u},x\right\rangle\right|\left|\left\langle x,(\theta-\hat{\theta})/\|\theta\|\right\rangle\right|\right)\leq 2\varepsilon.

Hence, the first term contributes
\leq CM\varepsilon
to both
T_{2}
and
T_{3}
.

It remains to bound the kink term involving
\mathbf{1}\{E_{\theta}\Delta E_{\hat{\theta}}\}/\|\theta\|
. If
E_{\theta}\Delta E_{\hat{\theta}}
occurs, then necessarily

\big|\left|\hat{z}\right|-\sqrt{M}\big|\leq\left|z-\hat{z}\right|=\left|\left\langle x,\theta-\hat{\theta}\right\rangle\right|
.
Thus for any
\tau\in(0,\sqrt{M}]
,

\mathbf{1}\{E_{\theta}\Delta E_{\hat{\theta}}\}\leq\mathbf{1}\{\big|\left|\hat{z}\right|-\sqrt{M}\big|\leq\tau\}+\mathbf{1}\{\left|\left\langle x,\theta-\hat{\theta}\right\rangle\right|\geq\tau\}.

Therefore, by using Cauchy-Schwarz with the identity
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{T}\preceq 2I
which holds with high probability we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\left\langle\hat{u},x_{i}\right\rangle\right|\frac{\mathbf{1}\{E_{\theta}(x_{i})\Delta E_{\hat{\theta}}(x_{i})\}}{\|\theta\|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle\hat{u},x_{i}\right\rangle^{2}}}{\|\theta\|}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\big|\left|\left\langle x_{i},\hat{\theta}\right\rangle\right|-\sqrt{M}\big|\leq\tau}\}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\left|\left\langle x_{i},\theta-\hat{\theta}\right\rangle\right|\geq\tau}\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\|\theta\|}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\big|\left|\left\langle x_{i},\hat{\theta}\right\rangle\right|-\sqrt{M}\big|\leq\tau}\}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\left\langle x_{i},\theta-\hat{\theta}\right\rangle^{2}}{\tau^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\|\theta\|}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\big|\left|\left\langle x_{i},\hat{\theta}\right\rangle\right|-\sqrt{M}\big|\leq\tau}\}+\frac{\sqrt{2}\|\theta-\hat{\theta}\|}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\|\theta\|}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\big|\left|\left\langle x_{i},\hat{\theta}\right\rangle\right|-\sqrt{M}\big|\leq\tau}\}+4\frac{\varepsilon}{\tau},

using
\|\theta-\hat{\theta}\|\leq 2\varepsilon\|\theta\|
.

Now we control the slab probability uniformly over
\hat{\theta}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
.
By Lemma
D.13
with
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
, with probability at least

1-2|\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}|\exp(-cn\tau/\sqrt{M})
we have

\sup_{\hat{\theta}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\{\left||\left\langle x_{i},\hat{\theta}\right\rangle|-\sqrt{M}\right|\leq\tau\}\}\leq\frac{\tau\|\hat{\theta}\|^{2}}{M^{3/2}}.

On this event,

\sup_{\theta,u}\frac{1}{\|\theta\|}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\big|\left|\left\langle x_{i},\hat{\theta}\right\rangle\right|-\sqrt{M}\big|\leq\tau}\}\leq\sup_{\hat{\theta}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{\|\hat{\theta}\|}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\big|\left|\left\langle x_{i},\hat{\theta}\right\rangle\right|-\sqrt{M}\big|\leq\tau}\}\leq C\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{M^{3/4}},

which implies that

\sup_{\theta,u}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\left\langle\hat{u},x_{i}\right\rangle\right|\frac{\mathbf{1}\{E_{\theta}(x_{i})\Delta E_{\hat{\theta}}(x_{i})\}}{\|\theta\|}\leq C\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{M^{3/4}}+C\frac{\varepsilon}{\tau}.

(D.24)

The population bounds are simpler: following the same passages and applying Lemma
D.12
gives

\sup_{\theta,u}\mathbb{E}\Big(\left|\left\langle\hat{u},x\right\rangle\right|\frac{\mathbf{1}\{E_{\theta}\Delta E_{\hat{\theta}}\}}{\|\theta\|}\Big)\leq C\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{M^{3/4}}+C\frac{\varepsilon}{\tau}.

(D.25)

Combining (
D.21
), (
D.22
), (
D.2.2
), (
D.24
), (
D.25
) and adding term (B), we have that on the good events

T_{2}+T_{3}\leq CM\eta+CM\varepsilon+CM^{3/4}\sqrt{\tau}+CM^{3/2}\frac{\varepsilon}{\tau}.

Let us now pick

\eta:=c_{1}\frac{\epsilon}{M},\qquad\tau:=\sqrt{M}\,\varepsilon^{2/3},\qquad\varepsilon:=c_{2}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{M}\right)^{3},

with small enough numerical constants
c_{1},c_{2}
so that
T_{2}+T_{3}\leq\epsilon/3
.
Here, the choice of
\tau=\sqrt{M}\varepsilon^{2/3}
balances the last two terms

M^{3/4}\sqrt{\tau}=M\varepsilon^{1/3}
and
M^{3/2}\varepsilon/\tau=M\varepsilon^{1/3}
.
Let us gather all the high probability events:

•

The failure probability of the event
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{T}\preceq 2I
is
\leq 2e^{-cd}
if
n\geq Cd
for a sufficiently large
C
.

•

T_{1}\leq\epsilon/3
from (
D.20
) with failure probability
2e^{-cd}
as long as

\displaystyle n\;\geq\;C\max\left(\frac{M^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{M}{\epsilon}\right)\Big(d\log\frac{3}{\varepsilon}+d\log\frac{3}{\eta}+\log(J+1)\Big),

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle n\;\geq\;C\max\left(\frac{M^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{M}{\epsilon}\right)\left(d\log\left(\frac{M}{\epsilon}\right)+\log(J+1)\right).

•

The slab-empirical event from Lemma
D.13
with
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
, which holds with probability at least

1-2|\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}|\exp(-cn\tau/\sqrt{M})\geq 1-2e^{-cd}.

Since
\tau/\sqrt{M}=\varepsilon^{2/3}=c_{2}^{2/3}\epsilon^{2}/M^{2}
, this requires

n\;\geq\;C\frac{M^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}\Big(d\log\frac{3}{\varepsilon}+\log(J+1)\Big).

These requirements are all of the form

\displaystyle n\geq C\frac{M^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}\left(d\log\left(\frac{M}{\epsilon}\right)+\log(J+1)\right),

where we recall that
J
is given by (
D.12
).
A union bound gives failure probability
\leq 6e^{-cd}
, and then
T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}\leq\epsilon
, proving (
D.17
) and concluding the argument.

∎

D.3
Norm growth during the first phase

Proposition D.14
.

Let
M
be a large enough constant (independent of
n,d
), and
C,c>0
be constants (independent of
n,d,M
). Consider the truncated quadratic activation in (
4.4
) and let
\theta_{t}
be obtained from the gradient descent iteration in (
4.2
), with learning rate
\eta\leq 1/(40\sqrt{2}M^{3/2})
and initialization norm
\|\theta_{0}\|\in[r,1/4)
. Assume that

n\geq CM^{2}\left(d\log M+\log\left(\log\left(\frac{10}{r}\right)+2\right)\right).

(D.26)

Let
t^{*}
be the smallest
t
such that
\|\theta_{t}\|\geq 1/4
. Then, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cd}
, the following results hold.

1.

Upper bound on
t^{*}
.
We have that

t^{*}\leq\left\lceil\frac{2\log\left(\frac{1}{4\|\theta_{0}\|}\right)}{\log\left(1+\frac{19}{25}\eta\right)}\right\rceil.

(D.27)

2.

Monotonic norm until
t^{*}
.
We have that
\|\theta_{t}\|>\|\theta_{t-1}\|
for
t\leq t^{*}
.

3.

Norm never below
t^{*}
again.
We have that
\|\theta_{t}\|\geq 1/4
for all
t\geq t^{*}
.

4.

Upper bound on the norm.
We have that
\|\theta_{t}\|\leq 10
for all
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

We start by showing that, uniformly over all
\theta
such that
\|\theta\|\in[r,1/2]
,

-\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta\rangle\geq\frac{19}{50}\|\theta\|^{2}.

(D.28)

To that aim, we write

-\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta\rangle\geq-\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\rangle-\|\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)-\mathcal{G}(\theta)\|\|\theta\|\geq-\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\rangle-\frac{1}{50}\|\theta\|^{2},

(D.29)

where the second inequality holds with probability at least
1-6e^{-cd}
uniformly over all
\theta
such that
\|\theta\|\in[r,1/2]
by Lemma
D.8
. To lower bound
\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\rangle
, we proceed as follows:

\begin{split}-\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\rangle&=-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sigma(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)-\sigma(\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle)\right)\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\right]\\
&=-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sigma(z)-\sigma(z^{\star})\right)\sigma^{\prime}(z)z\right]\\
&=2\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\sigma(z^{\star})z^{2}\mathbf{1}(|z|\leq\sqrt{M})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[z^{4}\mathbf{1}(|z|\leq\sqrt{M})\right]\right),\end{split}

(D.30)

where we have defined
z=\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle
and
z^{\star}=\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle
.
Let
\bar{r}=\|\theta\|
and
\rho=\left\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle/\|\theta\|
. Then, as
x
is a standard Gaussian vector, we have

z^{\star}=V,\qquad z=\bar{r}(\rho V+\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}W),

(D.31)

with
V,W
i.i.d. standard Gaussian.
We lower bound the first term in the RHS of (
D.30
) as

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}&\left[\sigma(z^{\star})z^{2}\mathbf{1}(|z|\leq\sqrt{M})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sigma(z^{\star})z^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\sigma(z^{\star})z^{2}\mathbf{1}(|z|>\sqrt{M})\right]\\
&\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\sigma(z^{\star})z^{2}\right]-M\mathbb{E}\left[z^{2}\mathbf{1}(|z|>\sqrt{M})\right]\\
&\geq\mathbb{E}\left[(z^{\star})^{2}z^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[(z^{\star})^{2}z^{2}\mathbf{1}(|z^{\star}|>\sqrt{M})\right]-M\mathbb{E}\left[z^{2}\mathbf{1}(|z|>\sqrt{M})\right].\end{split}

(D.32)

Next, we compute the first term in the RHS of (
D.32
) explicitly:

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[(z^{\star})^{2}z^{2}\right]&=\mathbb{E}\left[V^{2}\bar{r}^{2}(\rho V+\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}W)^{2}\right]=\bar{r}^{2}(1+2\rho^{2}).\end{split}

(D.33)

For the second term in the RHS of (
D.32
), we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[(z^{\star})^{2}z^{2}\mathbf{1}(|z^{\star}|>\sqrt{M})\right]&=\bar{r}^{2}\left(\rho^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[V^{4}\mathbf{1}(|V|>\sqrt{M})\right]+(1-\rho^{2})\mathbb{E}\left[V^{2}\mathbf{1}(|V|>\sqrt{M})\right]\right)\\
&\leq\bar{r}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{250}\rho^{2}+\frac{1}{5000}(1-\rho^{2})\right),\end{split}

(D.34)

where we have used that
E\left[V^{m}\mathbf{1}(|V|>\sqrt{M})\right]
(
m\in\{2,4\}
) is decreasing in
M
and
M\geq 20
. For the third term in the RHS of (
D.32
), note that
V^{\prime}:=\rho V+\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}W
is standard Gaussian. Thus, we have

\begin{split}M\mathbb{E}\left[z^{2}\mathbf{1}(|z|>\sqrt{M})\right]&=\bar{r}^{2}M\mathbb{E}\left[(V^{\prime})^{2}\mathbf{1}(|V^{\prime}|>\sqrt{M}/\bar{r})\right]\\
&\leq\bar{r}^{2}M\mathbb{E}\left[(V^{\prime})^{2}\mathbf{1}(|V^{\prime}|>2\sqrt{M})\right]\\
&\leq 10^{-6}\bar{r}^{2}.\end{split}

(D.35)

Here, in the second line we used that
E\left[(V^{\prime})^{2}\mathbf{1}(|V^{\prime}|>a)\right]
is decreasing in
a
and that
\bar{r}=\|\theta\|\leq 1/2
; in the third line, we used that the expression is decreasing in
M
and
M
is large enough.
Finally, we upper bound the second term in the RHS of (
D.30
) as

\mathbb{E}\left[z^{4}\mathbf{1}(|z|\leq\sqrt{M})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[z^{4}\right]=3\bar{r}^{4}\leq\frac{3}{4}\bar{r}^{2},

(D.36)

where in the last step we use that
\bar{r}=\|\theta\|\leq 1/2
.
By plugging these bounds into (
D.29
), we obtain

-\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta\rangle\geq\frac{2}{5}\|\theta\|^{2}-\frac{1}{50}\|\theta\|^{2},

(D.37)

which gives the claim in (
D.28
).

Now, if
r\leq\|\theta_{t}\|\leq 1/4\leq 1/2
, then

\begin{split}\|\theta_{t+1}\|^{2}=\langle\theta_{t}-\eta\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{t}),\theta_{t}-\eta\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{t})\rangle&=\|\theta_{t}\|^{2}-2\eta\langle\theta_{t},\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{t})\rangle+\eta^{2}\|\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{t})\|^{2}\\
&\geq\|\theta_{t}\|^{2}-2\eta\langle\theta_{t},\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{t})\rangle\\
&\geq\left(1+\frac{19}{25}\eta\right)\|\theta_{t}\|^{2}.\end{split}

(D.38)

Thus, as
r\leq\|\theta_{0}\|\leq 1/4
,
\|\theta_{t}\|
monotonically increases with
t
(in a strict way), until it becomes
\geq 1/4
for some
t^{*}
satisfying (
D.27
). This proves the first two claims.

Next, we show that
\|\theta_{t}\|\geq 1/4
for all
t\geq t^{*}
. Suppose, by contradiction, that this is not the case and let
\bar{t}
be the smallest integer larger than
t^{*}
such that
\|\theta_{\bar{t}}\|<1/4
. Note that

\begin{split}\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)\|&=\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{S}}^{d-1}}\langle u,\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)\rangle\\
&=\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{S}}^{d-1}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\sigma(\left\langle x_{i},\theta\right\rangle)-\sigma(\left\langle x_{i},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle))\,\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x_{i},\theta\right\rangle)\left\langle x_{i},u\right\rangle\\
&=\sqrt{\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\sigma(\left\langle x_{i},\theta\right\rangle)-\sigma(\left\langle x_{i},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle))^{2}(\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x_{i},\theta\right\rangle))^{2}}\sqrt{\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{S}}^{d-1}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle x_{i},u\right\rangle^{2}}\\
&\leq 2\sqrt{2}M^{3/2},\end{split}

(D.39)

where the third line follows from Cauchy-Schwarz and the last inequality uses that
(\sigma(\left\langle x_{i},\theta\right\rangle)-\sigma(\left\langle x_{i},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle))^{2}\leq M^{2}
,
(\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x_{i},\theta\right\rangle))^{2}\leq 4M
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and that
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\top}\preceq 2I
with probability at least
1-2e^{-cd}
when
n
is lower bounded as in (
D.26
). Let us express
\|\theta_{\bar{t}}\|
as

\begin{split}\|\theta_{\bar{t}}\|^{2}&=\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|^{2}-2\eta\langle\theta_{\bar{t}-1},\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{\bar{t}-1})\rangle+\eta^{2}\|\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{\bar{t}-1})\|^{2}\\
&\geq\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|^{2}-2\eta\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|\|\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{\bar{t}-1})\|\\
&\geq\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|^{2}-\frac{1}{10}\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|,\end{split}

(D.40)

where the third line uses (
D.39
) and that
\eta\leq 1/(40\sqrt{2}M^{3/2})
. As
\|\theta_{\bar{t}}\|<1/4
, we conclude that

\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|^{2}-\frac{1}{10}\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|-\frac{1}{16}<0,

which implies that

\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|<\frac{\frac{1}{10}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{100}+\frac{1}{4}}}{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

However, as
\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|\leq 1/2
, either
\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|
is in fact smaller than
r
or (
D.38
) holds with
t=\bar{t}-1
. In both cases,
\|\theta_{\bar{t}}\|\geq\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|
. Thus,
\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|<1/4
contradicting the minimality of
\bar{t}
. This proves the third claim.

It remains to show that
\|\theta_{t}\|\leq 10
for all
t
. To that aim, we start by showing that, uniformly over all
\theta
such that
\|\theta\|\in[r,10]
,

-\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta\rangle\leq\left(6+\frac{1}{50}\right)\|\theta\|^{2}-\frac{9}{5}\|\theta\|^{4}.

(D.41)

Note that

-\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta\rangle\leq-\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\rangle+\|\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)-\mathcal{G}(\theta)\|\|\theta\|\leq-\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\rangle+\frac{1}{50}\|\theta\|^{2},

(D.42)

where the second inequality holds with probability at least
1-6e^{-cd}
uniformly over all
\theta
such that
\|\theta\|\in[r,10]
by Lemma
D.8
.
For the term
-\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\rangle
, we use (
D.30
) and then obtain the following chain of inequalities:

\begin{split}-\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\rangle&=2\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\sigma(z^{\star})z^{2}\mathbf{1}(|z|\leq\sqrt{M})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[z^{4}\mathbf{1}(|z|\leq\sqrt{M})\right]\right)\\
&\leq 2\left(\mathbb{E}\left[(z^{\star})^{2}z^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[z^{4}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[z^{4}\mathbf{1}(|z|>\sqrt{M})\right]\right)\\
&=2\left(\bar{r}^{2}(3\rho^{2}+(1-\rho^{2}))-\bar{r}^{4}+\mathbb{E}\left[z^{4}\mathbf{1}(|z|>\sqrt{M})\right]\right)\\
&\leq 2\left(3\bar{r}^{2}-\bar{r}^{4}+\mathbb{E}\left[z^{4}\mathbf{1}(|z|>\sqrt{M})\right]\right).\end{split}

(D.43)

Furthermore, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[z^{4}\mathbf{1}(|z|>\sqrt{M})\right]&=\bar{r}^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[(V^{\prime})^{4}\mathbf{1}(|V^{\prime}|>\sqrt{M}/\bar{r})\right]\\
&=\bar{r}^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[(V^{\prime})^{4}\mathbf{1}(|V^{\prime}|>\sqrt{M}/10\right]\\
&\leq\frac{1}{10}\bar{r}^{4}.\end{split}

(D.44)

Here, in the second line we used that
E\left[(V^{\prime})^{4}\mathbf{1}(|V^{\prime}|>a)\right]
is decreasing in
a
and that
\bar{r}=\|\theta\|\leq 10
; in the third line, we used that the expression is decreasing in
M
and
M
is large enough. Combining (
D.42
), (
D.43
) and (
D.44
) gives (
D.41
).

Armed with this uniform bound, we now show that
\|\theta_{t}\|\leq 10
for all
t
. Suppose, by contradiction, that this is not the case and let
\bar{t}
be the smallest integer
\geq 1
such that
\|\theta_{\bar{t}}\|>10
. The minimality of
\bar{t}
readily implies that
\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|\leq 10
. Thus, we can apply the uniform bound we just proved to
\theta=\theta_{\bar{t}-1}
and obtain

\begin{split}\|\theta_{\bar{t}}\|^{2}&=\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|^{2}-2\eta\langle\theta_{\bar{t}-1},\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{\bar{t}-1})\rangle+\eta^{2}\|\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{\bar{t}-1})\|^{2}\\
&\leq\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|^{2}+\left(6+\frac{1}{50}\right)\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|^{2}-\frac{9}{5}\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|^{4}+\eta^{2}\|\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{\bar{t}-1})\|^{2}\\
&\leq\left(7+\frac{1}{50}\right)\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|^{2}-\frac{9}{5}\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|^{4}+\frac{1}{100},\end{split}

(D.45)

where in the last step we use (
D.39
) and that
\eta\leq 1/(40\sqrt{2}M^{3/2})
. Now, the RHS of (
D.45
) is
<100
for any
\|\theta_{\bar{t}-1}\|
, which gives the desired contradiction and concludes the proof.
∎

D.4
Angle does not decrease

Proposition D.15
.

Fix any
\varphi\in(0,\pi/4)
. Let
M
be a large enough constant (independent of
n,d
), and
C,c>0
be constants (independent of
n,d,M
, but possibly dependent on
\varphi
). Consider the truncated quadratic activation in (
4.4
). Let
\theta_{t}
be obtained from the gradient descent iteration in (
4.2
), with learning rate
\eta\leq 1/(40\sqrt{2}M^{3/2})
and initialization norm
\|\theta_{0}\|\in[r,1/4)
. Assume that (
D.26
) holds.
Let
t^{*}
be the smallest
t
such that
\angle(\theta_{t},\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
. Then, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cd}
,
\angle(\theta_{t},\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
for all
t\geq t^{*}
.

We start by noting that there exist scalars
A(\theta,\theta^{\star}),B(\theta,\theta^{\star})
such that

\mathcal{G}(\theta)=A(\theta,\theta^{\star})\,\theta-B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\,\theta^{\star}.

(D.46)

Lemma D.16
.

For every
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

B(\theta,\theta^{\star})=\mathbb{E}\big[\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle)\big].

(D.47)

In particular, for
\sigma
given by (
4.4
),

B(\theta,\theta^{\star})=4\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\,\mathbf{1}\{|\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle|<\sqrt{M},\ |\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle|<\sqrt{M}\}\Big].

(D.48)

Proof.

Define
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by

f(x):=(\sigma(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)-\sigma(\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle))\,\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle).

Then by (
4.7
),
\mathcal{G}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}[x\,f(x)]
. Thus, by Stein’s lemma

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x)]=A(\theta,\theta^{\star})\theta-\mathbb{E}\big[\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle)\big]\theta^{\star}.

Extracting the coefficient of
\theta^{\star}
gives
B
and comparing with (
D.46
) yields (
D.47
). Substituting
\sigma^{\prime}(z)=2z\mathbf{1}\{|z|<\sqrt{M}\}
yields (
D.48
).
∎

Lemma D.17

(Bounds on
A(\theta,\theta^{\star})
and
B(\theta,\theta^{\star})
)
.

Let
M\geq 1
and
\phi:=\angle(\theta,\theta^{\star})\in[0,\pi]
so that
\left\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle=\|\theta\|\cos\phi
. Then, the following results hold.

1.

Uniform upper bound on
A(\theta,\theta^{\star})
.
For every
\theta\neq 0
,

|A(\theta,\theta^{\star})|\leq 4M+4.

(D.49)

2.

Lower bound on
B(\theta,\theta^{\star})
.
For every
\theta
,

B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\ \geq\ 4\,\|\theta\|\left(\cos\phi-\sqrt{3}\,\sqrt{\,2\frac{\|\theta\|}{\sqrt{M}}e^{-\frac{M}{2\|\theta\|^{2}}}+2e^{-\frac{M}{2}}\,}\right).

(D.50)

Proof.

We use the identity

\left\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\right\rangle=A(\theta,\theta^{\star})\|\theta\|^{2}-B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\left\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle,

(D.51)

which follows by taking the inner product of (
D.46
) with
\theta
. Rearranging gives, for
\theta\neq 0
,

A(\theta,\theta^{\star})=\frac{\left\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\right\rangle+B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\left\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle}{\|\theta\|^{2}}.

(D.52)

Upper bound on
\left\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\right\rangle
.

By (
4.7
),

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[(\sigma(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)-\sigma(\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle))\,\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)\,\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\Big].

As
0\leq\sigma(z)\leq M
for all
z
, we have

\big|\sigma(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)-\sigma(\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle)\big|\leq M.

Furthermore,
\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)=2\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\mathbf{1}\{|\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle|<\sqrt{M}\}
, hence

\big|\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)\,\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\big|=2\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle^{2}\,\mathbf{1}\{|\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle|<\sqrt{M}\}\leq 2\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle^{2},

which implies

\big|(\sigma(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)-\sigma(\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle))\,\sigma^{\prime}(\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle)\,\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\big|\leq 2M\cdot(2\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle^{2})=4M\,\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle^{2}.

Taking expectations and using
\mathbb{E}[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle^{2}]=\|\theta\|^{2}
yields

\big|\left\langle\mathcal{G}(\theta),\theta\right\rangle\big|\leq 4M\,\|\theta\|^{2}.

(D.53)

Upper bound on
|B(\theta,\theta^{\star})|
.

From (
D.48
),

|B(\theta,\theta^{\star})|=\left|4\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\,\mathbf{1}\{|\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle|<\sqrt{M},\ |\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle|<\sqrt{M}\}\Big]\right|\leq 4\,\mathbb{E}\big|\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\big|.

By Cauchy–Schwarz,

\mathbb{E}\big|\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\big|\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle^{2}\big]\mathbb{E}\big[\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle^{2}\big]}.

Now
\mathbb{E}[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle^{2}]=\|\theta\|^{2}
,
\mathbb{E}[\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle^{2}]=\|\theta^{\star}\|^{2}=1
. Therefore,

|B(\theta,\theta^{\star})|\leq 4\,\|\theta\|.

(D.54)

Upper bound on
A(\theta,\theta^{\star})
.

Using (
D.52
), (
D.53
),
|\left\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle|\leq\|\theta\|
, and (
D.54
),

|A(\theta,\theta^{\star})|\leq\frac{4M\|\theta\|^{2}}{\|\theta\|^{2}}+\frac{|B(\theta,\theta^{\star})|\,|\left\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle|}{\|\theta\|^{2}}\leq 4M+\frac{(4\|\theta\|)\cdot\|\theta\|}{\|\theta\|^{2}}=4M+4,

proving (
D.49
).

Lower bound on
B(\theta)
.

Let

E:=\{|\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle|<\sqrt{M},\ |\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle|<\sqrt{M}\}.

Then by (
D.48
),

B(\theta,\theta^{\star})=4\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\,\mathbf{1}\{E\}\Big]=4\left(\mathbb{E}[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle]-\mathbb{E}\Big[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\,\mathbf{1}\{E^{c}\}\Big]\right).

Hence, using
z\geq-|z|
,

B(\theta)\geq 4\left(\mathbb{E}[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle]-\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\big|\,\mathbf{1}\{E^{c}\}\Big]\right).

We have
\mathbb{E}[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle]=\left\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle=\|\theta\|\cos\phi
.
Also by applying Cauchy-Schwarz twice,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\big|\,\mathbf{1}\{E^{c}\}\Big]&\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle^{2}\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle^{2}\big]}\,\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(E^{c})}\\
&\leq\sqrt[4]{\mathbb{E}\big[\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle^{4}\big]\mathbb{E}\big[\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle^{4}\big]}\,\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(E^{c})}\\
&\leq\sqrt{3}\,\|\theta\|\,\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(E^{c})}.\end{split}

(D.55)

Therefore

B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\geq 4\|\theta\|\Big(\cos\phi-\sqrt{3}\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(E^{c})}\Big).

(D.56)

Finally, by the union bound,

\mathbb{P}(E^{c})\leq\mathbb{P}(|\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle|\geq\sqrt{M})+\mathbb{P}(|\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle|\geq\sqrt{M}).

Since
\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\|\theta\|^{2})
and
\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, the standard Gaussian tail bound gives

\mathbb{P}(|\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle|\geq\sqrt{M})\leq 2\frac{\|\theta\|}{\sqrt{M}}\exp\!\left(-\frac{M}{2\|\theta\|^{2}}\right),\qquad\mathbb{P}(|\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle|\geq\sqrt{M})\leq 2\exp\!\left(-\frac{M}{2}\right).

Substituting into (
D.56
) yields (
D.50
).
∎

Lemma D.18
.

Fix
\varphi\in(0,\pi/2)
. Let
\theta\neq 0
satisfy
\angle(\theta,\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
, where
\|\theta^{\star}\|=1
. Let the population gradient
\mathcal{G}(\theta)
admit the decomposition

\mathcal{G}(\theta)=A(\theta,\theta^{\star})\,\theta-B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\,\theta^{\star}\quad\text{with}\quad B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\geq 0,

and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)
be any perturbed gradient satisfying

\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)-\mathcal{G}(\theta)\|\leq\Delta:=B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\,\frac{\tan\varphi}{1+\tan\varphi}.

(D.57)

Consider one step
\theta^{+}=\theta-\eta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)
, where the step size
\eta>0
satisfies

1-\eta A(\theta,\theta^{\star})\geq 0.

(D.58)

Then,
\angle(\theta^{+},\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
.

Proof.

Let
e:=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)-\mathcal{G}(\theta)
, so

\|e\|\leq\Delta
.
Consider the
population step
vector

\bar{\theta}^{+}:=\theta-\eta\mathcal{G}(\theta)=\theta-\eta\big(A(\theta,\theta^{\star})\theta-B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\theta^{\star}\big)=\alpha\,\theta+\eta B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\theta^{\star},

where
\alpha:=1-\eta A(\theta,\theta^{\star})\geq 0
by (
D.58
). Then, we have

\theta^{+}=\theta-\eta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)=\theta-\eta(\mathcal{G}(\theta)+e)=\bar{\theta}^{+}-\eta e.

Let
\Pi_{\perp}:=I-\theta^{\star}(\theta^{\star})^{\top}
denote the projection onto
(\theta^{\star})^{\perp}
. For any nonzero vector
z
with
\left\langle z,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle>0
,

\tan(\angle(z,\theta^{\star}))=\frac{\|\Pi_{\perp}z\|}{\left\langle z,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle}.

Lower bound on the new parallel component.

Set
\phi:=\angle(\theta,\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
. By Cauchy-Schwarz,

\left\langle\theta^{+},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle=\left\langle\bar{\theta}^{+}-\eta e,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle=\left\langle\bar{\theta}^{+},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle-\eta\left\langle e,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\geq\left\langle\bar{\theta}^{+},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle-\eta\|e\|\geq\left\langle\bar{\theta}^{+},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle-\eta\Delta.

Moreover,

\left\langle\bar{\theta}^{+},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle=\left\langle\alpha\theta+\eta B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\theta^{\star},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle=\alpha\,\left\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle+\eta B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\geq\alpha\|\theta\|\cos\phi+\eta B(\theta,\theta^{\star}),

since
\alpha\geq 0
and
\left\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\geq 0
whenever
\angle(\theta,\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi<\pi/2
. Hence, we have

\left\langle\theta^{+},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\geq\alpha\|\theta\|\cos\phi+\eta(B(\theta,\theta^{\star})-\Delta).

(D.59)

As
\frac{\tan\varphi}{1+\tan\varphi}<1
,
\Delta<B(\theta,\theta^{\star})
, hence
\left\langle\theta^{+},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle>0
and the tangent formula applies to
\theta^{+}
.

Upper bound on the new orthogonal component.

Using the triangle inequality and
\|\Pi_{\perp}e\|\leq\|e\|
,

\|\Pi_{\perp}\theta^{+}\|=\|\Pi_{\perp}(\bar{\theta}^{+}-\eta e)\|\leq\|\Pi_{\perp}\bar{\theta}^{+}\|+\eta\|\Pi_{\perp}e\|\leq\|\Pi_{\perp}\bar{\theta}^{+}\|+\eta\Delta.

Since
\Pi_{\perp}\theta^{\star}=0
,

\|\Pi_{\perp}\bar{\theta}^{+}\|=\|\Pi_{\perp}(\alpha\theta+\eta B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\theta^{\star})\|=\|\alpha\,\Pi_{\perp}\theta\|=\alpha\,\|\Pi_{\perp}\theta\|.

We have
\|\Pi_{\perp}\theta\|=\|\theta\|\sin\phi
. Therefore,

\|\Pi_{\perp}\theta^{+}\|\leq\alpha\,\|\theta\|\sin\phi+\eta\Delta.

(D.60)

Bound on the new angle.

Combining (
D.59
) and (
D.60
),

\tan(\angle(\theta^{+},\theta^{\star}))=\frac{\|\Pi_{\perp}\theta^{+}\|}{\left\langle\theta^{+},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle}\leq\frac{\alpha\|\theta\|\sin\phi+\eta\Delta}{\alpha\|\theta\|\cos\phi+\eta(B(\theta,\theta^{\star})-\Delta)}.

To ensure
\angle(\theta^{+},\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
, it suffices that the right-hand side is at most
\tan\varphi
:

\frac{\alpha\|\theta\|\sin\phi+\eta\Delta}{\alpha\|\theta\|\cos\phi+\eta(B(\theta,\theta^{\star})-\Delta)}\leq\tan\varphi.

Multiplying by the positive denominator and rearranging yields

\alpha\|\theta\|\sin\phi+\eta\Delta\leq\alpha\|\theta\|\cos\phi\tan\varphi+\eta\tan\varphi\,B(\theta,\theta^{\star})-\eta\tan\varphi\,\Delta.

Rearranging the above the latter is equivalent to

\displaystyle\eta\Delta(1+\tan\varphi)\leq

\displaystyle\alpha\|\theta\|(\cos\phi\tan\varphi-\sin\phi)+\eta\tan\varphi\,B(\theta,\theta^{\star})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha\|\theta\|\frac{\sin(\varphi-\phi)}{\cos\varphi}+\eta\tan\varphi\,B(\theta,\theta^{\star})

Since
\phi\leq\varphi
the first term is positive and a valid sufficient condition is

\eta\Delta(1+\tan\varphi)\leq\eta B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\tan\varphi,

which is satisfied by our choice of
\Delta
in (
D.57
). This proves
\angle(\theta^{+},\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
and concludes the argument.
∎

Lemma D.19
.

Let
M\geq 1
and
\theta\neq 0
satisfy
\angle(\theta,\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi<\pi/2
.
Let
\theta^{+}=\theta-\eta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)
, where
\eta\leq 1/(4M+4)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)
is any perturbation of the population gradient
\mathcal{G}(\theta)
in (
4.7
) satisfying

\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)-\mathcal{G}(\theta)\|\leq\left[4\,\left(\cos\varphi-\sqrt{3}\,\sqrt{\,2\frac{R}{\sqrt{M}}e^{-\frac{M}{2R^{2}}}+2e^{-\frac{M}{2}}\,}\right)\right]\cdot\frac{\tan\varphi}{1+\tan\varphi}\|\theta\|\quad\text{for all }\theta\text{ s.t.}\quad r\leq\|\theta\|\leq R.

(D.61)

Then, one has
\angle(\theta^{+},\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
.

Proof.

By Lemma
D.17
,
A(\theta,\theta^{\star})\leq 4M+4
, so the assumption on the step size
\eta\leq\frac{1}{4M+4}
implies
1-\eta A(\theta,\theta^{\star})\geq 0
.
By assumption, (
D.61
) holds and note that

\begin{split}&\left[4\,\left(\cos\varphi-\sqrt{3}\,\sqrt{\,2\frac{R}{\sqrt{M}}e^{-\frac{M}{2R^{2}}}+2e^{-\frac{M}{2}}\,}\right)\right]\cdot\frac{\tan\varphi}{1+\tan\varphi}\|\theta\|\\
&\qquad\leq\left[4\,\|\theta\|\left(\cos(\angle(\theta,\theta^{\star}))-\sqrt{3}\,\sqrt{\,2\frac{\|\theta\|}{\sqrt{M}}e^{-\frac{M}{2\|\theta\|^{2}}}+2e^{-\frac{M}{2}}\,}\right)\right]\cdot\frac{\tan\varphi}{1+\tan\varphi}\\
&\qquad\leq B(\theta,\theta^{\star})\cdot\frac{\tan\varphi}{1+\tan\varphi}:=\Delta,\end{split}

(D.62)

where in the first inequality we use that
\angle(\theta,\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
and
\|\theta\|\leq R
, and in the second inequality we use the lower bound (
D.50
) on
B(\theta,\theta^{\star})
from Lemma
D.17
. Thus, condition (
D.57
) holds and the result follows from Lemma
D.18
.
∎

At this point, the desired result showing that the angle does not decrease is a consequence of Lemmas
D.19
and
D.8
.

Proof of Proposition
D.15
.

We verify that the hypotheses of Lemma
D.19
hold when taking
\theta=\theta_{t}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)=\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{t})
, where
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta_{t})
is the empirical gradient in (
4.3
). The upper bound
\eta\leq 1/(4M+4)
required by Lemma
D.19
is implied by the upper bound
\eta\leq 1/(40\sqrt{2}M^{3/2})
for large enough
M
. By Proposition
D.14
, we have that
\|\theta_{t}\|\in[r,10]
, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cd}
uniformly for all
t\geq 0
. Hence, we can take
R=10
. For large enough
M
,

\cos\varphi-\sqrt{3}\,\sqrt{\,2\frac{R}{\sqrt{M}}e^{-\frac{M}{2R^{2}}}+2e^{-\frac{M}{2}}\,}\geq\cos\varphi-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cos\varphi,

where the last inequality holds for all
\varphi\in[0,\pi/4]
.
Then, an application of Lemma
D.8
with
\epsilon:=2\cos\varphi\frac{\tan\varphi}{1+\tan\varphi}
gives that (
D.61
) holds, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cd}
. In fact, the lower bound on
n
in (
D.10
) is implied by that in (
D.26
) (for a suitable choice of
C
depending on
\varphi
). Then, the conclusion of Lemma
D.19
holds: if
\angle(\theta_{t},\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
, then
\angle(\theta_{t+1},\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cd}
uniformly for all
t\geq 0
. This is equivalent to the desired claim and the proof is complete.
∎

D.5
Uniform concentration of the Gram matrix of the Jacobian

For
\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let us define the random matrix

\widehat{H}(\theta,\widetilde{\theta}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x_{i},\theta\rangle)\,\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x_{i},\widetilde{\theta}\rangle)\,x_{i}x_{i}^{\top},\qquad H:=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{H}(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})].

(D.63)

Lemma D.20
.

Let
\widehat{H}(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})
be defined in (
D.63
) and the annulus
\Theta
be defined in (
D.9
). Fix
\epsilon\in(0,1/2)
and let
M
be sufficiently large. There exist constants
c,C>0
such that if

n\ \geq\ C\,d\ \frac{M^{2}\log^{2}\left(\frac{eM}{\epsilon}\right)}{\epsilon^{2}},

(D.64)

then with probability at least
1-3e^{-cd}
,

\sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}\ \|\widehat{H}(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})-H(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})\|\ \leq\ \epsilon.

(D.65)

D.5.1
Preliminary results

We start by providing some auxiliary probabilistic results.
Recall that a real-valued random variable
X
is said to be sub-exponential with parameters

(\sigma^{2},b)
if, for all
|\lambda|\leq\frac{1}{b}
,

\mathbb{E}\exp\!\big(\lambda(X-\mathbb{E}X)\big)\;\leq\;\exp\!\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}\right).

(D.66)

For such subexponential random variables, we have the following standard refined Bernstein-type inequality, see Eq. (2.18) in
(
72
)
.

Theorem D.21

(Bernstein’s-type inequality for sub-exponential sums)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent mean-zero random variables, where each
X_{i}
is
sub-exponential with parameters
(\sigma^{2},b)
in the sense of
(
D.66
). Then, for every
s\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\geq s\right)\;\leq\;2\exp\!\left(-\frac{1}{2}n\cdot\min\left\{\frac{s^{2}}{\sigma^{2}},\;\frac{s}{b}\right\}\right).

(D.67)

Lemma D.22
.

Let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
Y=(Z^{2}-\tau)_{+}
, where
[\cdot]_{+}=\max(\cdot,0)
. Then,
Y
is sub-exponential with parameters
(8,128e^{-\tau/2})
.

Proof.

Note that, for
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}(Z^{2}>t)\leq 2e^{-t/2},

which implies that

\mathbb{P}(Y\geq t)\leq 2e^{-t/2-\tau/2}.

Thus, we obtain that, for any integer
k\geq 1
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[Y^{k}]&=\int_{0}^{\infty}kt^{k-1}\mathbb{P}(Y\geq t)dt\\
&\leq 2e^{-\tau/2}k\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k-1}e^{-t/2}dt\\
&\leq k!2^{k+1}e^{-\tau/2}.\end{split}

(D.68)

Let
X:=Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]
. Then,

\mathbb{E}[|X|^{k}]\leq 2^{k-1}\left(\mathbb{E}[Y^{k}]+(\mathbb{E}[Y])^{k}\right)\leq 2^{k}\mathbb{E}[Y^{k}]\leq k!2^{2k+1}e^{-\tau/2},

(D.69)

where the second passage uses Jensen inequality.

We now claim that, for a random variable
X
with zero mean, if there exists constants
v^{2}>0
and
c>0
such that, for every integer
k\geq 2
,

\mathbb{E}[|X|^{k}]\leq\frac{k!}{2}v^{2}c^{k-2},

(D.70)

then for every
\lambda<1/c
,

\log\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda X}]\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}v^{2}}{2(1-c|\lambda|)}.

(D.71)

In particular, for
|\lambda|\leq 1/(2c)
, we have

\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda X}]\leq e^{\lambda^{2}v^{2}},

which means that
X
is sub-exponential with parameters
b=2c
and
\sigma^{2}=2v^{2}
. Thus, from the bound on the moments in (
D.69
), we deduce that (
D.70
) holds with
c=4
and
v^{2}=64e^{-\tau/2}
and, therefore, the desired result holds with
b=8
and
\sigma^{2}=128e^{-\tau/2}
.

It remains to prove the claim in (
D.71
). To that aim,
note that

\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda X}]=1+\lambda\mathbb{E}[X]+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}[X^{k}]=1+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}[X^{k}].

Upper bounding
\mathbb{E}[X^{k}]
by
\mathbb{E}[|X^{k}|]
and using (
D.70
) gives

\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda X}]\leq 1+\frac{v^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}(c|\lambda|)^{k-2}=1+\frac{v^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2(1-c|\lambda|)},

where the last passage holds as
|\lambda|<1/c
. Taking the
\log
on both sides and using that
\log(1+u)\leq u
for all
u\geq 0
concludes the argument.
∎

We will use the following result on covering numbers for VC-subgraph classes.

Theorem D.23

(Theorem 2.6.7 in
(
69
)
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a VC-subgraph class of real-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with VC-subgraph dimension at most
V
and envelope bound
|h|\leq B
pointwise.
Then there exist absolute constants
A,C>0
such that for every probability measure
Q
and every
0<\eta\leq B
,

\log N(\eta,\mathcal{H},L_{2}(Q))\ \leq\ CV\log\!\Bigl(\frac{AB}{\eta}\Bigr).

We will also use the following Dudley-type bound for Rademacher averages.

Theorem D.24

(Equation (5.48) in
(
72
)
)
.

There exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that, for any function class
\mathcal{F}
,
conditionally on
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}f(x_{i})\Big|\Big]\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{0}^{2r}\sqrt{\log N(\eta,\mathcal{F},L_{2}(P_{n}))}\,d\eta,

where
P_{n}
is the empirical measure of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
and
r:=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\|f\|_{L_{2}(P_{n})}
.

Finally, we will use Bousquet’s concentration inequality for suprema of bounded empirical processes.

Theorem D.25

(Theorem 2.3 in
(
12
)
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a class of measurable functions with
0\leq f\leq b
.
Let

Z:=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(f(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}f(X)\big),\qquad\sigma^{2}:=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{Var}(f(X)).

Then for all
t\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-e^{-t}
,

Z\leq\mathbb{E}Z+\sqrt{2t(n\sigma^{2}+2b\mathbb{E}Z)}+\frac{b\,t}{3}.

(D.72)

In particular, we will further upper bound the RHS of (
D.72
) as

\mathbb{E}Z+\sqrt{2t(n\sigma^{2}+2b\mathbb{E}Z)}+\frac{b\,t}{3}\leq 2\mathbb{E}Z+\sqrt{2tn\sigma^{2}}+\frac{4b\,t}{3},

(D.73)

where we have used that
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
.

D.5.2
Proof of Lemma
D.20

The argument of this lemma is partly inspired by and generalizes the results of
74
which develops spectral norm concentration guarantees for
\widehat{H}
for the ReLU activation (i.e.,
\sigma={\rm ReLU}
).
Let
\mathcal{U}_{1/4}
be a
1/4
-net of the unit sphere. Then, by Corollary 4.2.13 of
(
70
)
, we have that
|\mathcal{U}_{1/4}|\leq 9^{d}
and by Lemma 4.4.1 in
(
70
)
, we have

\begin{split}\left\|\widehat{H}(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})-H(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})\right\|&=\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{S}}^{d-1}}\left|u^{\top}(\widehat{H}(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})-H(\theta,\widetilde{\theta}))u\right|\\
&\leq\ \frac{1}{(1-1/4)^{2}}\ \max_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}}\left|u^{\top}(\widehat{H}(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})-H(\theta,\widetilde{\theta}))u\right|\\
&\leq 2\max_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}}\left|u^{\top}(\widehat{H}(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})-H(\theta,\widetilde{\theta}))u\right|.\end{split}

(D.74)

For fixed
u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}
and
\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta
, define the scalar function

f_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x):=\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\theta\rangle)\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\widetilde{\theta}\rangle)\,\langle u,x\rangle^{2}.

Let
P_{n}
be the empirical measure and
P
the law of
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
.
Then, we have

u^{\top}(\widehat{H}(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})-H(\theta,\widetilde{\theta}))u=(P_{n}-P)f_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x_{i})-\mathbb{E}[f_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x)]\right).

Thus, it suffices to bound

\max_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}}\ \sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}\ |(P_{n}-P)f_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}|.

To do so, we truncate the term
\langle u,x\rangle^{2}
. Fix
\tau\geq 1
.
For
t\geq 0
, define

T_{\tau}(t):=\min\{t,\tau\},\qquad R_{\tau}(t):=(t-\tau)_{+}.

For
u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}
, define

W_{u}(x):=\langle u,x\rangle^{2},\qquad W_{u}^{(\tau)}(x):=T_{\tau}(W_{u}(x)),\qquad G_{u}^{(\tau)}(x):=R_{\tau}(W_{u}(x)).

Then,
W_{u}=W_{u}^{(\tau)}+G_{u}^{(\tau)}
, and therefore

f_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x)=f^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x)+r^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x),

where

f^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x):=\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\theta\rangle)\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\widetilde{\theta}\rangle)\,W_{u}^{(\tau)}(x),\qquad r^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x):=\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\theta\rangle)\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\widetilde{\theta}\rangle)\,G_{u}^{(\tau)}(x).

Thus, we have

\sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}|(P_{n}-P)f_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}|\leq\sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}|(P_{n}-P)f^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}|+\sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}|(P_{n}-P)r^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}|.

(D.75)

We start by bounding the second term in the RHS of (
D.75
). To that aim, first note that, for all
x
,
\theta
, and
\widetilde{\theta}
,

\left|\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\theta\rangle)\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\widetilde{\theta}\rangle)\right|\leq(2\sqrt{M})(2\sqrt{M})=4M,

(D.76)

which implies that

|r^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x)|\leq 4M\,G_{u}^{(\tau)}(x),

namely this term is automatically uniform in
\theta,\widetilde{\theta}
.
Using
|(P_{n}-P)h|\leq P_{n}|h|+P|h|
for any
h
, we have

\sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}|(P_{n}-P)r^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}|\leq 4M\left(P_{n}G_{u}^{(\tau)}+PG_{u}^{(\tau)}\right).

We now claim that, for any
\tau\geq 1
, there exist absolute constants
c,C>0
such that with probability at least
1-2e^{-cd}
,

\max_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{u}^{(\tau)}(x_{i})\ \leq\ Ce^{-c\tau}\ +\ C\sqrt{\frac{e^{-c\tau}\,d}{n}}\ +\ C\frac{d}{n}.

(D.77)

Proof of the claim in (
D.77
).

Fix
u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}
. Since
\left\langle x,u\right\rangle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, we have
G_{u}^{(\tau)}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(Z^{2}-\tau)_{+}
, with
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
By Lemma
D.22
,
G_{u}^{(\tau)}(x)

is sub-exponential with parameters
(\sigma^{2},b)
, with
\sigma^{2}\leq Ce^{-c\tau}
and
b\leq C
. Thus, an application of Theorem
D.21
with
s=C\sqrt{\frac{e^{-c\tau}t}{n}}+C\frac{t}{n}
yields for all
t\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{u}^{(\tau)}(x_{i})-\mathbb{E}G_{u}^{(\tau)}(x)\geq C\sqrt{\frac{e^{-c\tau}t}{n}}+C\frac{t}{n}\right)\leq 2e^{-t}.

Set
t=c_{1}d
and do a union bound over
|\mathcal{U}_{1/4}|\leq 9^{d}
; choosing
c_{1}
large enough makes the union-bound failure probability
\leq 2e^{-cd}
and concludes the proof. ∎

From (
D.68
) with
k=1
, we also have that

\displaystyle PG_{u}^{(\tau)}\leq Ce^{-c\tau},

which combined with (
D.77
) gives that

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}|(P_{n}-P)r^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}|\leq CMe^{-c\tau}+CM\sqrt{\frac{e^{-c\tau}\,d}{n}}\ +\ CM\frac{d}{n}.

(D.78)

We now bound the first term in the RHS of (
D.75
). To that aim, fix
u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}
and
\tau\geq 1
.
Define the class

\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau}:=\left\{f^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(\cdot):\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta\right\}.

As
0\leq W_{u}^{(\tau)}\leq\tau
and (
D.76
) holds, we have the uniform bound

\bigl|f^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x)\bigr|\leq 4M\tau,\qquad\text{for all $x,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}$.}

(D.79)

We further decompose

f^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x)=m_{u,\tau}(x)\,g_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x),

where
g_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}(x):=\langle x,\theta\rangle\langle x,\widetilde{\theta}\rangle\,\mathbf{1}\{|x^{\top}\theta|<\sqrt{M}\}\mathbf{1}\{|x^{\top}\widetilde{\theta}|<\sqrt{M}\}
is the
(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})
-dependent factor and
m_{u,\tau}(x):=4W_{u}^{(\tau)}(x)
is fixed (for this choice of
u,\tau
). Note that
|m_{u,\tau}(x)|\leq 4\,\tau
and
|g_{\theta}(x)|\leq M
.
Hence, for any probability measure
Q
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\theta,\theta^{\prime},\widetilde{\theta},\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime}
,

\|f^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}-f^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta^{\prime},\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime}}\|_{L_{2}(Q)}\leq 4\tau\ \|g_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}-g_{\theta^{\prime},\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime}}\|_{L_{2}(Q)}.

(D.80)

Consequently, for every
\eta>0
,

N(\eta,\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau},L_{2}(Q))\leq N\!\left(\frac{\eta}{4\tau},\mathcal{G},L_{2}(Q)\right),\qquad\mathcal{G}:=\{g_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}:\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}.

(D.81)

Note that the class
\mathcal{G}:=\{g_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}:\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
is VC-subgraph with VC-subgraph dimension
V\leq Cd
for an absolute constant
C
. To prove this claim, we write out the set
G
of subgraphs of the class
{\mathcal{G}}
, which is by definition

G\coloneqq\left\{\mathrm{subgraph}(g_{\theta,\tilde{\theta}})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,g_{\theta,\tilde{\theta}}\in{\mathcal{G}}\right\},\text{ where }\ \mathrm{subgraph}(g_{\theta,\tilde{\theta}})=\left\{(x,\xi)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\exists\xi\leq g_{\theta,\tilde{\theta}}(x)\right\}.

A membership test for an arbitrary
\mathrm{subgraph}(g_{\theta,\tilde{\theta}})\in G
can be expressed as intersection of
5
polynomial inequalities of degree at most
2
in
x
and
\xi
, i.e.,

(x,\xi)\in\mathrm{subgraph}(g_{\theta,\tilde{\theta}})\Longleftrightarrow-\sqrt{M}\leq x^{\top}\theta\leq\sqrt{M}\ \wedge\ -\sqrt{M}\leq x^{\top}\theta\leq\sqrt{M}\ \wedge\ \xi\leq(x^{\top}\theta)(x^{\top}\tilde{\theta}).

The result of
(
37
, Theorem 2.2)
then implies that
\VC(G)\leq Cd
, for
C
an absolute constant, proving that
{\mathcal{G}}
is VC-subgraph with VC-subgraph dimension
V\leq Cd
.

Thus, applying Theorem
D.23
to
\mathcal{G}
with
B=M
and
V\leq Cd
, we obtain that, for all
Q
and
0<\eta\leq M
,

\log N(\eta,\mathcal{G},L_{2}(Q))\leq Cd\log\!\Bigl(\frac{AM}{\eta}\Bigr).

Using (
D.81
), we deduce that, for all
Q
and all
0<\eta\leq 4M\tau
,

\log N(\eta,\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau},L_{2}(Q))\leq Cd\log\!\Bigl(\frac{A^{\prime}M\tau}{\eta}\Bigr),

(D.82)

for an absolute constant
A^{\prime}>0
.

Let
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
be i.i.d. Rademacher signs independent of the data. By symmetrization (see e.g. Lemma 2.3.1 in
(
69
)
with
\Phi(t)=t
), we have

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau}}|(P_{n}-P)f|\Big]\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}f(x_{i})\Big|\Big].

(D.83)

Applying Theorem
D.24
with
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau}
,
\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau}}\|f\|_{\infty}\leq b:=4M\tau
from (
D.79
) and the entropy bound (
D.82
) with
Q=P_{n}
, we obtain (conditionally on the samples
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
)

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}f(x_{i})\Big|\Big]\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{0}^{2b}\sqrt{d\log\!\Bigl(\frac{A^{\prime}M\tau}{\eta}\Bigr)}\,d\eta.

Using the bound

\int_{0}^{2b}\sqrt{\log\Big(\frac{A^{\prime}M\tau}{\eta}\Big)}\,d\eta\ \leq\ C\,b\,\sqrt{\log\Big(\frac{eA^{\prime}M\tau}{b}\Big)}\leq C^{\prime}b,

we conclude that

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}f(x_{i})\Big|\Big]\leq C\,b\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}.

Combining with (
D.83
) yields

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}|(P_{n}-P)f^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}|\Big]\leq C\,(4M\tau)\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}.

(D.84)

We now apply Theorem
D.25
to two shifted classes to handle two-sided deviations.
Define
\mathcal{F}^{+}:=\{(f+b)/2:\ f\in\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau}\}
and
\mathcal{F}^{-}:=\{(-f+b)/2:\ f\in\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau}\}
.
Note that
0\leq(\pm f+b)/2\leq b
and

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau}}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f(x_{i})-\mathbb{E}f)\right|\leq 2\max\{Z^{+},Z^{-}\},

where
Z^{\pm}
are the suprema for
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}
.
Combining Theorem
D.25
(and more precisely the upper bound in (
D.73
)) with
\sigma^{2}\leq\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}^{\pm}}\mathbb{E}[g^{2}]\leq b^{2}
, dividing by
n
and using
\mathbb{E}Z^{\pm}\leq(n/2)\mathbb{E}[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{u,\tau}}|(P_{n}-P)f|]
,
together with (
D.84
), we obtain:

\sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}|(P_{n}-P)f^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}|\leq C\,b\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}+C\,b\sqrt{\frac{t}{n}}+C\,b\frac{t}{n},\qquad b=4M\tau,

(D.85)

for each fixed
u
and all
t\geq 1
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-t}
.
Now choose
t=c_{0}d
with
c_{0}
large enough and take a union bound over
u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}
recalling that
|\mathcal{U}_{1/4}|\leq 9^{d}
.
Thus,

\max_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}}\ \sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}|(P_{n}-P)f^{(\tau)}_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}|\leq C\,b\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}+C\,b\frac{d}{n},\qquad b=4M\tau,

(D.86)

with probability at least
1-e^{-cd}
.
Combining (
D.75
), (
D.78
) and (
D.86
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\max_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}}\ \sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}|(P_{n}-P)f_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}|\leq C(4M\tau)\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}+C(4M\tau)\frac{d}{n}+CM\left(e^{-c\tau}+\sqrt{\frac{e^{-c\tau}\,d}{n}}\ +\ \frac{d}{n}\right),

with probability at least
1-3e^{-cd}
.
Using (
D.74
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}\|\widehat{H}(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})-H(\theta,\widetilde{\theta})\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{1/4}}\ \sup_{\theta,\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta}|(P_{n}-P)f_{u,\theta,\widetilde{\theta}}|

\displaystyle\leq C(M\tau)\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}+C(M\tau)\frac{d}{n}+CM\sqrt{\frac{e^{-c\tau}d}{n}}+CM\frac{d}{n}+CMe^{-c\tau}.

(D.87)

Setting
\tau:=C_{0}\log\!\Bigl(\frac{eM}{\epsilon}\Bigr)
with
C_{0}
large enough so that
CMe^{-c\tau}\leq\epsilon/10
and using (
D.64
) concludes the proof.
∎

D.6
Exponential convergence during the second phase

Proposition D.26
.

Let
M
be a large enough constant,
r\leq 1/4
and fix
\varphi\leq cr^{2}/M
for a small enough constant
c
(independent of
n,d,M,r,R
). Consider the parameter set

\Theta_{\varphi}:=\Bigl\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}:\ \angle(\theta,\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi,\ r\leq\|\theta\|\leq R\Bigr\}.

(D.88)

Assume that

n\ \geq\ Cd\frac{M^{2}}{r^{4}}\log^{2}\left(\frac{M}{r^{2}}\right),

(D.89)

for a large enough constant
C
(independent of
n,d,M,r,R
). Consider the truncated quadratic activation in (
4.4
), and let
\theta_{t}
be obtained from the gradient descent iteration in (
4.2
), with learning rate
\eta\leq cr^{2}/M^{2}
. Assume further that there exists
\bar{t}
such that, for all
t\geq\bar{t}
,
\theta_{t}\in\Theta_{\varphi}
. Then, with probability at least
1-7e^{-cd}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\theta_{t}-\theta^{\star}\|^{2}\leq{\left(1-\eta\alpha\right)}^{t-\bar{t}}\|\theta_{\tau}-\theta^{\star}\|^{2},

with
\alpha=cr^{2}
.

We start with a preliminary geometric lemma. For
a,b\in{\mathcal{S}}^{d-1}
, define the sign-disagreement event

D(a,b)\;=\;\{x:\ \operatorname{sign}(\left\langle x,a\right\rangle)\neq\operatorname{sign}(\left\langle x,b\right\rangle)\}.

Let us also define
\mathcal{S}(\theta):=\{x:\left\langle x,\theta\right\rangle\,\left\langle x,\theta^{\star}\right\rangle<0\}
and note that
\mathcal{S}(\theta)=D(\theta,\theta^{\star})
.

Lemma D.27
.

Let
g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, and let
a,b,u\in{\mathcal{S}}^{d-1}
. Set
\alpha=\angle(a,b)\in[0,\pi]
.
Then

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\langle g,u\rangle^{2}\,\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}\right]\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\,\alpha.

Proof.

Let
S=\mathrm{span}\{a,b\}
have dimension
\dim S
, and write the orthogonal decompositions

u=u_{S}+u_{\perp},\qquad g=g_{S}+g_{\perp},

with
u_{S},g_{S}\in S
and
u_{\perp},g_{\perp}\in S^{\perp}
, where
S^{\perp}
denotes the subspace orthogonal to
S
. Since
g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, we have
that
g_{S}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{\dim S})
is independent of
g_{\perp}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d-\dim S})
.
Note that
D(a,b)
depends only on
\langle g,a\rangle,\langle g,b\rangle
and hence only on
g_{S}
.

If
\dim S\leq 1
, then
a=\pm b
, which implies that
\alpha\in\{0,\pi\}
. For
\alpha=0
,
\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}=0
, hence
both expectations are
0
and the bounds hold. For
\alpha=\pi
,
\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}=1
,
\mathbb{E}\!\left[\langle g,u\rangle^{2}\right]=1
,
\mathbb{E}\!\left[\langle g,u\rangle^{4}\right]=3
, hence the bounds hold. Thus, we assume below that
\dim S=2
(equivalently

\alpha\in(0,\pi)
). Define

X:=\langle g_{S},u_{S}\rangle,\qquad Y:=\langle g_{\perp},u_{\perp}\rangle,

so that
\langle g,u\rangle=X+Y
. Note that
Y
is independent of
(X,\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\})
and satisfies

\mathbb{E}[Y]=0
,
\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}]=\|u_{\perp}\|^{2}
, and
\mathbb{E}[Y^{4}]=3\|u_{\perp}\|^{4}
.

By rotational invariance in the plane
S
, choose an orthonormal basis of
S
such that

a=e_{1},\qquad b=\cos\alpha\,e_{1}+\sin\alpha\,e_{2}.

Let us write
g_{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
in polar form

g_{S}=r(\cos\theta,\sin\theta),

where
\theta\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,2\pi)
is independent of
r\geq 0
, and
r^{2}\sim\chi^{2}_{2}
. In particular,

\mathbb{E}[r^{2}]=2,\qquad\mathbb{E}[r^{4}]=8.

Furthermore, we have

\langle g_{S},a\rangle=r\cos\theta,\qquad\langle g_{S},b\rangle=r\cos(\theta-\alpha),

which implies that

D(a,b)=\{\cos\theta\cdot\cos(\theta-\alpha)<0\}.

Note that
D(a,b)
depends on
\theta
and not on
r
.
On the circle this set consists of two disjoint angular intervals, each of length
\alpha
, hence

\mathbb{P}(g\in D(a,b))=\frac{2\alpha}{2\pi}=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}.

(D.90)

Using that
Y
is independent of
(X,\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\})
and

\mathbb{E}[Y]=0
, we obtain

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[(X+Y)^{2}\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}]&=\mathbb{E}[X^{2}\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}]+2\mathbb{E}[XY\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}]+\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}]\\
&=\mathbb{E}[X^{2}\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}]+\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}]\mathbb{P}(g\in\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}).\end{split}

Next, we write
u_{S}=\|u_{S}\|_{2}(\cos\beta,\sin\beta)
for some
\beta
. Then, we have

X=\langle g_{S},u_{S}\rangle=r\|u_{S}\|\cos(\theta-\beta),

and therefore

X^{2}\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}=r^{2}\|u_{S}\|^{2}\cos^{2}(\theta-\beta)\,\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}.

Using the independence of
r
and
\theta
and the fact that
\cos^{2}\gamma\leq 1
for all
\gamma
, we have

\mathbb{E}[X^{2}\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}]=\mathbb{E}[r^{2}]\|u_{S}\|^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\cos^{2}(\theta-\alpha)\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}[r^{2}]\|u_{S}\|^{2}\,\mathbb{P}(g\in\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\})=2\|u_{S}\|^{2}\frac{\alpha}{\pi},

where we used (
D.90
) in the last step. Hence,

\mathbb{E}[\langle g,u\rangle^{2}\mathbf{1}\{D(a,b)\}]\leq\left(2\|u_{S}\|^{2}+\|u_{\perp}\|^{2}\right)\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\,\alpha,

since
\|u_{S}\|^{2}+\|u_{\perp}\|^{2}=\|u\|^{2}=1
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
D.26
.

Proof of Proposition
D.26
.

First we show that

\displaystyle\|\nabla\mathcal{L}(\theta)\|\leq L\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|,

with
L:=4M
. To do this, note that using Cauchy-Schwarz and the fact that
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\top}\preceq 2I
with probability at least
1-2e^{-cd}
we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla\mathcal{L}(\theta)\|=

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in{\mathcal{S}}^{d-1}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sigma(\langle x_{i},\theta\rangle)-\sigma(\langle x_{i},\theta^{\star}\rangle)\right)\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x_{i},\theta\rangle)(\langle x_{i},u\rangle)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{M}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sigma(\langle x_{i},\theta\rangle)-\sigma(\langle x_{i},\theta^{\star}\rangle)\right)^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\langle x_{i},u\rangle)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2M}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sigma(\langle x_{i},\theta\rangle)-\sigma(\langle x_{i},\theta^{\star}\rangle)\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{8}M\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\langle x_{i},\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4M\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|,

where in the penultimate step we used the fact that
\sigma
is
2\sqrt{M}
-Lipschitz. The key part of the argument is to show the one-point strong convexity

\displaystyle\left\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta-\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\geq\alpha\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|^{2},

(D.91)

with
\alpha=cr^{2}
for some numerical constant
c>0
independent of
n,d,M,r,R
.
Having established (
D.91
), if we let
\theta
and
\theta^{+}=\theta-\eta\nabla\mathcal{L}(\theta)
be two subsequent gradient descent iterations such that
\theta,\theta^{+}\in\Theta_{\varphi}
, we have

\begin{split}\|\theta^{+}-\theta^{\star}\|^{2}=&\|\theta-\theta^{\star}-\eta\nabla\mathcal{L}(\theta)\|^{2}\\
=&\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|^{2}-2\eta\langle\mathcal{L}(\theta),\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle+\eta^{2}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}(\theta)\|^{2}\\
\leq&\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|^{2}-2\alpha\eta\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|^{2}+\eta^{2}L^{2}\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|^{2}\\
\leq&\left(1-\alpha\eta\right)\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|^{2},\end{split}

(D.92)

where in the last line we used the fact
\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{L^{2}}
. Hence, the desired result follows by iterating (
D.92
).

The rest of the argument consists in proving (
D.91
). Let us write the inner product

\displaystyle\left\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta-\theta^{\star}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sigma\left(\langle x_{i},\theta\rangle\right)-\sigma(\langle x_{i},\theta^{\star}\rangle)\right)\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x_{i},\theta\rangle)\langle x_{i},\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle.

(D.93)

Now we apply the integral form of the mean value theorem

\displaystyle\sigma(\langle x_{i},\theta\rangle)-\sigma(\langle x_{i},\theta^{\star}\rangle)=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\sigma^{\prime}\left(\langle x_{i},\theta^{\star}+t(\theta-\theta^{\star})\rangle dt\right)\right)\langle x_{i},\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle.

Thus,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle=

\displaystyle(\theta-\theta^{\star})^{\top}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma^{\prime}\left(\langle x_{i},\theta^{\star}+t(\theta-\theta^{\star})\rangle dt\right)\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x_{i},\theta\rangle)x_{i}x_{i}^{\top}\right)dt\right)(\theta-\theta^{\star})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\theta-\theta^{\star})^{\top}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{H}(\theta^{\star}+t(\theta-\theta^{\star}),\theta)dt\right)(\theta-\theta^{\star}),

where
\hat{H}
is the Gram matrix of the Jacobian defined in (
D.63
).
Therefore,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta)

\displaystyle,\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle-\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle=(\theta-\theta^{\star})^{\top}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left(\widehat{H}(\theta^{\star}+t(\theta-\theta^{\star}),\theta)-\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{H}(\theta^{\star}+t(\theta-\theta^{\star}),\theta)\big]\right)dt\right)(\theta-\theta^{\star}).

We lower bound the quantity
\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle\Big]
.
To that aim, let us define the event

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\{\sgn(\langle x,\theta\rangle)=\sgn(\langle x,\theta^{\star}\rangle)\},

and decompose (
D.93
) based on the alignment of the signs into two terms

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle\Big]=&\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sigma\left(\langle x,\theta\rangle\right)-\sigma(\langle x,\theta^{\star}\rangle)\right)\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\theta\rangle)\langle x,\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{S}^{c}\}\right]\\
&+\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sigma\left(\langle x,\theta\rangle\right)-\sigma(\langle x,\theta^{\star}\rangle)\right)\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\theta\rangle)\langle x,\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{S}\}\right].\end{split}

(D.94)

Towards bounding the first term in the RHS of (
D.94
), we write

\displaystyle\big|\left(\sigma\left(\langle x,\theta\rangle\right)-\sigma(\langle x,\theta^{\star}\rangle)\right)\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\theta\rangle)\langle x,\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle\big|\leq 4M\langle x,\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2},

where we have used that
\sigma
is
2\sqrt{M}
-Lipschitz. Thus, we obtain the lower bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sigma\left(\langle x,\theta\rangle\right)-\sigma(\langle x,\theta^{\star}\rangle)\right)\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\theta\rangle)\langle x,\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{S}^{c}\}\right]

\displaystyle\geq-4M\mathbb{E}\left[\langle x,\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{S}^{c}\}\right]\geq-\frac{8M}{\pi}\varphi\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|^{2},

(D.95)

where the last passage follows from Lemma
D.27
.

Next, we claim the following lower bound on the second term of the RHS in (
D.94
):

\mathbb{E}\Big[(\sigma(\langle x,\theta\rangle)-\sigma(\langle x,\theta^{\star}\rangle))\,\sigma^{\prime}(\langle x,\theta\rangle)\,(\langle x,\theta\rangle-\langle x,\theta^{\star}\rangle)\,\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{S}\}\Big]\ \geq\ \frac{f(1/4)f(1/8)}{2^{19}}\,r^{2}(\cos\varphi-\sin\varphi)^{2}\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|_{2}^{2},

(D.96)

where
f(u)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-u^{2}/2}
is the standard normal density.

Proof of the claim in (
D.96
).

Let us write

a=\langle x,\theta\rangle,\qquad b=\langle x,\theta^{\star}\rangle,\qquad u=\theta-\theta^{\star},

and note that the LHS of (
D.96
) can be expressed as

Q:=\mathbb{E}\Big[(\sigma(a)-\sigma(b))\,\sigma^{\prime}(a)\,(a-b)\,\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{S}\}\Big].

As
a
and
b
have the same sign on
\mathcal{S}
, we have
(\sigma(a)-\sigma(b))\,\sigma^{\prime}(a)\,(a-b)\geq 0
. Thus, adding an extra indicator only reduces
Q
and we obtain the lower bound

\displaystyle Q\geq\mathbb{E}\Big[(\sigma(a)-\sigma(b))\,\sigma^{\prime}(a)\,(a-b)\,\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{S}\}\mathbf{1}\{\left|a\right|\leq\sqrt{M},\left|b\right|\leq\sqrt{M}\}\Big].

On the event
\{|a|<\sqrt{M},\ |b|<\sqrt{M}\}
we have
\sigma(a)=a^{2}
,
\sigma(b)=b^{2}
, and
\sigma^{\prime}(a)=2a
, hence

(\sigma(a)-\sigma(b))\,\sigma^{\prime}(a)\,(a-b)=(a^{2}-b^{2})\cdot 2a\cdot(a-b)=2a(a+b)(a-b)^{2}.

On
\mathcal{S}=\{ab\geq 0\}
, we further obtain that

a(a+b)=a^{2}+ab\geq a^{2}.

Consequently, on
\{|a|<\sqrt{M},\ |b|<\sqrt{M}\}\cap\mathcal{S}
,

(\sigma(a)-\sigma(b))\,\sigma^{\prime}(a)\,(a-b)\ \geq\ 2a^{2}(a-b)^{2}.

Thus

Q\ \geq\ 2\,\mathbb{E}\Big[a^{2}(a-b)^{2}\,\mathbf{1}\{\{|a|<\sqrt{M},\ |b|<\sqrt{M}\}\cap\mathcal{S}\}\Big].

Let
\phi:=\angle(\theta,\theta^{\star})\leq\varphi
and
r\leq\|\theta\|\leq R
.
Focusing on the
2
-dimensional subspace spanned by
\theta^{\star}
and
\theta
, pick an orthonormal basis
(e_{1},e_{2})
for this plane with
e_{1}=\theta^{\star}
. Then, we can
write

x=g_{1}e_{1}+g_{2}e_{2}+x_{\perp},

where
g_{1},g_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
x_{\perp}
is independent of
(g_{1},g_{2})
.
Thus,

b=g_{1},\qquad a=\|\theta\|(\cos\phi\,g_{1}+\sin\phi\,g_{2}).

Define the event

\mathcal{B}:=\left\{g_{1}\in\left[\frac{1}{8},\frac{1}{4}\right],\ \ |g_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{8}\right\}.

On
\mathcal{B}
, we have

\cos\phi\,g_{1}+\sin\phi\,g_{2}\ \geq\ \frac{1}{8}(\cos\phi-\sin\phi)\ >\ 0\qquad(\text{since }\varphi<\pi/4),

hence
a>0
and also
b>0
. Therefore
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{S}
.
Moreover, on
\mathcal{B}
, using the assumption on
M\geq 16R^{2}
and
R\geq 1
, we have

|b|=|g_{1}|\leq\frac{1}{4}\leq\sqrt{M},

and

|a|\leq\|\theta\|(|g_{1}|+|g_{2}|)\leq R\cdot\frac{3}{8}\leq\sqrt{M},

so
\mathcal{B}\subset\{|a|<\sqrt{M},\ |b|<\sqrt{M}\}
. Finally, still on
\mathcal{B}
,

a\ \geq\frac{\|\theta\|}{8}(\cos\phi-\sin\phi)\ \geq\ r\cdot\frac{1}{8}(\cos\varphi-\sin\varphi),

and therefore

a^{2}\ \geq\ \frac{r^{2}}{64}(\cos\varphi-\sin\varphi)^{2}.

Thus, using
\mathcal{B}\subset\{|a|<\sqrt{M},|b|<\sqrt{M}\}\cap\mathcal{S}
yields

Q\ \geq\ 2\,\mathbb{E}\Big[a^{2}(a-b)^{2}\,\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}\Big]\ \geq\ \frac{r^{2}}{32}(\cos\varphi-\sin\varphi)^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[(a-b)^{2}\,\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}\Big].

Note that
a-b=\langle x,u\rangle
. Let us decompose
u=u_{\parallel}+u_{\perp}
where

u_{\parallel}\in\mathrm{span}\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
and
u_{\perp}\perp\mathrm{span}\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
. We also write
u_{\parallel}=u_{1}e_{1}+u_{2}e_{2}
, which gives that

\langle x,u\rangle=u_{1}g_{1}+u_{2}g_{2}+\langle x_{\perp},u_{\perp}\rangle.

Note that
\langle x_{\perp},u_{\perp}\rangle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\|u_{\perp}\|_{2}^{2})
is independent of
(g_{1},g_{2})
and hence independent of
\mathcal{B}
.
Therefore, we have

\mathbb{E}[(a-b)^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}]=\mathbb{E}[(\langle x,u\rangle)^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}]\geq\mathbb{E}[(u_{1}g_{1}+u_{2}g_{2})^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}].

Since
\mathcal{B}
is symmetric in
g_{2}
,
\mathbb{E}[g_{1}g_{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}]=0
, and hence

\mathbb{E}[(u_{1}g_{1}+u_{2}g_{2})^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}]=u_{1}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}]+u_{2}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[g_{2}^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}].

We now lower bound the two expectations. Because the standard normal density
f
is decreasing on
[0,\infty)
,

\mathbb{P}(g_{1}\in[1/8,1/4])=\int_{1/8}^{1/4}f(s)\,ds\ \geq\ \frac{1}{8}\,f(1/4),\qquad\mathbb{P}(|g_{2}|\leq 1/8)=\int_{-1/8}^{1/8}f(s)\,ds\ \geq\ \frac{1}{4}\,f(1/8).

Hence

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B})\ \geq\ \frac{1}{32}\,f(1/4)f(1/8).

On
\mathcal{B}
we have
g_{1}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{64}
, so

\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}]\ \geq\ \frac{1}{64}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B})\ \geq\ \frac{1}{2048}\,f(1/4)f(1/8).

For
g_{2}
, define the subset

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}:=\{g_{1}\in[1/8,1/4],\ |g_{2}|\in[1/16,1/8]\}\subset\mathcal{B}.

On
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
we have
g_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{16^{2}}
. Also,

\mathbb{P}(|g_{2}|\in[1/16,1/8])=2\int_{1/16}^{1/8}f(s)\,ds\ \geq\ 2\cdot\frac{1}{16}\,f(1/8)=\frac{1}{8}\,f(1/8),

so

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\geq\mathbb{P}(g_{1}\in[1/8,1/4])\cdot\mathbb{P}(|g_{2}|\in[1/16,1/8])\geq\frac{1}{8}\phi(1/4)\cdot\frac{1}{8}f(1/8)=\frac{1}{64}f(1/4)f(1/8).

Therefore

\mathbb{E}[g_{2}^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}]\ \geq\ \mathbb{E}[g_{2}^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\}]\ \geq\ \frac{1}{16^{2}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\ \geq\ \frac{1}{16384}\,f(1/4)f(1/8).

Combining the two bounds gives

\mathbb{E}[(a-b)^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{B}\}]\geq\frac{1}{16384}\,f(1/4)f(1/8)\,(u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2})\geq\frac{1}{16384}\,f(1/4)f(1/8)\,\|u\|^{2}.

This allows us to conclude that

Q\geq\frac{r^{2}}{32}(\cos\varphi-\sin\varphi)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{16384}f(1/4)f(1/8)\,\|u\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{f(1/4)f(1/8)}{524288}\,r^{2}(\cos\varphi-\sin\varphi)^{2}\,\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|_{2}^{2},

which is the bound in (
D.96
). ∎

We are now ready to put everything together and conclude the proof. By combining (
D.94
), (
D.95
) and (
D.96
), we have

\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle\Big]\geq\left(c_{1}r^{2}-\frac{8M}{\pi}\varphi\right)\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|_{2}^{2},

(D.97)

where we have used that, as
\varphi
is small,
\cos\varphi-\sin\varphi
is lower bounded by a strictly positive numerical constant
c_{0}
and
c_{1}:=c_{0}f(1/4)f(1/8)/524288
is also a strictly positive numerical constant (independent of
n,d,M,r,R
). Next, we apply Proposition
D.20
with
\epsilon=c_{1}r^{2}/2
(which is possible due to the lower bound on
n
in (
D.89
)) and therefore we conclude that, with probability at least
1-3e^{-cd}
,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\theta),\theta-\theta^{\star}\rangle\geq\left(\frac{c_{1}}{2}r^{2}-\frac{8M}{\pi}\varphi\right)\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\geq c_{2}r^{2}\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|_{2}^{2},

(D.98)

where in the last step we use that
\varphi\leq cr^{2}/M
for a small enough numerical constant
c
. This proves the one-point strong convexity in (
D.91
) and concludes the argument.
∎

D.7
Patching the two phases and concluding the argument

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

As
\eta\leq c/M^{2}
and
\delta\geq CM^{4}
, we can apply Proposition
D.1
, which gives that, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{d^{2}}
,

\angle(\theta_{t^{*}_{\measuredangle}},\theta^{\star})\leq C_{1}(e^{-M/2}+M\delta^{-1/2}),\qquad\mbox{with }t^{*}_{\measuredangle}=\frac{3\log d}{\log(1+1.99\eta)},

(D.99)

for numerical constants
c_{1},C_{1}>0
(independent of
n,d,M,\delta
). Furthermore, the constraints
\eta\leq c/M^{2}
and
\delta\geq CM^{4}
also allow to apply Proposition
D.14
with
r=d^{-15}
, which gives that, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cd}
,

\|\theta_{t}\|\geq 1/4,\qquad\mbox{for all }t\geq t^{*}_{\rm norm}:=\left\lceil\frac{2\log\left(\frac{1}{4\|\theta_{0}\|}\right)}{\log\left(1+\frac{19}{25}\eta\right)}\right\rceil,

(D.100)

and that

\|\theta_{t}\|\leq 10,\qquad\mbox{for all }t\geq 0.

(D.101)

Next, we apply Proposition
D.15
with
r=d^{-15}
,
\varphi=C_{1}(e^{-M/2}+M\delta^{-1/2})
and
t^{*}=t^{*}_{\measuredangle}
, which gives that, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cd}
,

\angle(\theta_{t},\theta^{\star})\leq C_{1}(e^{-M/2}+M\delta^{-1/2}),\qquad\mbox{for all }t\geq t^{*}_{\measuredangle}.

(D.102)

Note that, as
\delta\geq CM^{4}
for a large enough constant
C
, the upper bound on
\angle(\theta_{t},\theta^{\star})
in (
D.102
) can be made
c_{2}/M
for any small constant
c_{2}
.
Hence, we can apply Proposition
D.26
with
r=1/4
,
R=10
,
\varphi=c_{2}/M
and
\bar{t}=\max(t^{*}_{\measuredangle},t^{*}_{\rm norm})
, obtaining that, for all
t\geq\bar{t}
, with probability at least
1-7e^{-cd}
,

\displaystyle\|\theta_{t}-\theta^{\star}\|^{2}\leq{\left(1-\eta\alpha\right)}^{t-\bar{t}}\|\theta_{\bar{t}}-\theta^{\star}\|^{2},

with
\alpha>0
a numerical constant independent of
n,d,M,\delta
. Note that
\|\theta_{\bar{t}}-\theta^{\star}\|^{2}\leq 202
by using (
D.101
). Furthermore, we have that
\max(t^{*}_{\measuredangle},t^{*}_{\rm norm})\leq C\log d/\eta
. Thus, a union bound on all these high-probability events gives the desired claim with probability at least
1-2/d^{2}
, thus concluding the argument.
∎
